14. Q: What should happen when one source uses a different account hierarchy from the NGO's chart of accounts?
A: Show answer
The source hierarchy should be mapped into the organization's approved chart rather than copied directly into the general ledger reporting structure. First identify whether the source account is more detailed, less detailed, or conceptually different. Then create a mapping that shows the source account, target account, mapping level, effective date, and review status. If several source accounts legitimately roll into one target account, the mapping can be many-to-one, but the organization should confirm that this does not remove information needed for management or donor reporting. Power Query can apply the mapping consistently across all imported records. The mapping should be tested on historical samples before production use because an incorrect hierarchy can systematically misclassify large volumes of transactions.

Study instruction:
- "Practice assessing the level of detail in source accounts before creating a target chart-of-accounts mapping."
Key point:
- Source hierarchies do not have to match the reporting chart exactly.
- Many source accounts can map to one target category when the aggregation remains meaningful.

15. Q: How would you distinguish a project code from a grant code when one grant funds several projects?
A: Show answer
The grant and project should normally remain separate dimensions if the organization needs to report both award-level funding and operational activity. One grant can then legitimately relate to several projects, while each project remains separately identifiable. A transaction may therefore contain both the project code and grant code, and the relationship between them can be validated through a grant-project eligibility table. This prevents a common error where staff assume that the project name alone tells Finance which donor award should be charged. Power Query can merge the transaction with the approved grant-project relationship and flag combinations that are not permitted. The control should focus on whether the combination is valid, not merely whether each code is valid independently.

Study instruction:
- "Learn how relationship tables can validate combinations of otherwise valid codes."
Key point:
- A valid project and a valid grant are not automatically a valid combination.
- Grant-project relationships should be explicitly controlled.
Source links:
- Merge Queries Overview — Microsoft Learn
- Create a Data Model in Excel — Microsoft Support
16. Q: Why is combination validation important when several coding dimensions are used together?
A: Show answer
Individual codes can be valid while the combination is wrong. A project may be active, the grant may be active, and the account may be valid, but the grant may not fund that project or the account may be prohibited under the applicable budget structure. Combination validation therefore checks relationships rather than isolated values. A coding matrix can define permitted project-grant-account combinations or more manageable compatibility rules. Power Query can merge transactions against relationship tables and produce invalid combinations as exceptions. This is particularly valuable where restrictions or donor reporting structures make classification dependent on more than one dimension. Without combination validation, a system may accept technically valid codes that produce materially misleading reports.

Study instruction:
- "Study the difference between validating individual codes and validating permitted code combinations."
Key point:
- Valid components do not automatically create a valid transaction classification.
- Relationship controls add a second level of coding quality.
Source links:
- Merge Queries Overview — Microsoft Learn
- Create a Data Model in Excel — Microsoft Support
17. Q: How would you handle transactions that legitimately relate to more than one project or grant?
A: Show answer
I would not create an arbitrary single code simply to fit a one-row structure. Where the underlying cost is shared and policy allows allocation, the transaction should be split using a documented allocation basis, such as approved usage, headcount, time records, activity measures, or another rational driver appropriate to the cost. The allocation rule should be documented and applied consistently. If a source system cannot split the transaction, the transformation layer can create allocation rows before the final reporting output, provided the resulting entries remain traceable to the original transaction. VBA can generate or validate the allocation schedule, while Power Query can consolidate the allocated rows back into project and grant reports. The important control is that the allocation is evidence-based rather than based on convenience.

Study instruction:
- "Review how shared costs should be allocated using a documented and repeatable basis rather than arbitrary percentages."
Key point:
- Shared transactions may require controlled allocation rather than forced single coding.
- The allocation basis should be documented and reproducible.
Source links:
- Combine CSV Files in Power Query — Microsoft Learn
- Merge Queries Overview — Microsoft Learn
18. Q: How would you allocate common costs across projects without creating arbitrary coding?
A: Show answer
First identify the economic reason the cost is shared and then choose an allocation driver that reasonably reflects that cause. For example, a shared support service might be allocated using documented usage or another approved basis, while a directly attributable purchase should normally remain directly charged to the benefiting project. The allocation method should be approved, documented, and applied consistently over the relevant period unless circumstances change. A workbook should preserve the original transaction identifier and show the allocation calculation so reviewers can trace how the split was created. Power Query can apply a maintained allocation table, while VBA can calculate and check that allocated amounts equal the source total. A good control always preserves the link between the original cost and its allocated outputs.

Study instruction:
- "Learn to choose allocation bases that reflect the reason a cost is shared and retain the calculation evidence."
Key point:
- Allocation should follow a documented rationale.
- Allocated totals must reconcile back to the original source transaction.

19. Q: What would you check when a transaction has a valid account code but the wrong project code?
A: Show answer
I would first determine whether the account classification is correct and then trace the project coding back to the originating evidence. Useful documents may include the purchase request, budget approval, activity plan, contract, invoice, travel authorization, or project manager confirmation. I would check whether the error occurred during data entry, source-system extraction, mapping, or the original transaction approval. The correction should preserve the original transaction reference and document who approved the reclassification and why. If the same problem appears repeatedly, I would not treat each row as an isolated correction; I would investigate the underlying mapping or training weakness. Power Query can identify recurring combinations and VBA can summarize them for management review.

Study instruction:
- "Practice tracing a coding error through its source, mapping, approval, and correction stages."
Key point:
- Correct the transaction and investigate why the error occurred.
- Repeated coding errors may indicate a process problem rather than isolated user mistakes.

20. Q: How would you identify duplicate transactions created when several source files are combined?
A: Show answer
I would establish a transaction key using fields such as source system, source transaction ID, date, amount, reference, and other identifying attributes appropriate to the data. The exact key should be designed carefully because identical amounts and dates do not necessarily prove duplication. Power Query can remove exact duplicates or flag possible duplicates for review rather than automatically deleting them. The source file name and source row information should be retained so Finance can trace a questionable record back to its origin. Where two systems legitimately report the same economic event, the reconciliation logic must distinguish duplicate representation from duplicate accounting. This is one reason a source-system identifier is more useful than relying solely on amount matching.

Study instruction:
- "Design a duplicate-detection key that balances automation with enough evidence for human review."
Key point:
- Duplicate detection should be based on a controlled matching rule.
- Potential duplicates should be reviewed before deletion or exclusion.

21. Q: Why should every imported transaction retain its source identifier?
A: Show answer
A standardized reporting table is much easier to audit when every row can be traced back to its source record. The source identifier might be an invoice number, bank transaction ID, payroll reference, journal number, purchase-order number, or partner report reference. Where a source does not provide a unique identifier, the organization can create a controlled composite key while preserving the original source fields. Power Query can retain source file information when combining files, allowing reviewers to identify where a row originated. Maintaining this link makes coding corrections easier because Finance can return to the underlying evidence instead of reconstructing the transaction from a summarized report.

Study instruction:
- "Make source traceability a required field in the reporting dataset."
Key point:
- Every reporting row should be traceable to a source record.
- Traceability reduces investigation time and strengthens review.

22. Q: How would you handle transactions from bank statements when the bank does not provide project or grant codes?
A: Show answer
Bank data usually identifies the cash movement but may not contain enough information to determine the full accounting classification. I would therefore treat bank information as one source of evidence rather than as the sole coding source. The bank transaction should retain its reference, date, amount, and description, then be matched to the underlying payment request, invoice, journal, transfer, or other transaction record that contains the project and grant information. Where a reliable matching rule exists, Power Query can automate the match using reference numbers or other identifiers. Unmatched bank items should remain visible on a reconciliation or coding exception list until the appropriate supporting information is obtained.

Study instruction:
- "Understand why cash movement data often needs to be combined with transaction-support data before full coding can be determined."

- A bank statement confirms cash movement, not necessarily full accounting classification.

23. Q: How would you maintain consistent coding for payroll across many projects and grants?
A: Show answer
Payroll coding should be driven by the approved employee, position, project, grant, and allocation structure rather than by whichever code happens to be typed into a monthly payroll file. Where employees work across projects, the approved allocation basis should be maintained separately and then applied to payroll data. A payroll master can define employee identifiers, roles, home department, default coding, and approved project or grant allocations. Power Query can merge payroll results with the allocation table and produce project-level payroll records. The process should also include checks for staff who have changed projects, ended assignments, or lack a current allocation. This prevents old project codes from continuing automatically after responsibilities have changed.

Study instruction:
- "Review how payroll coding can be controlled through employee allocation tables and effective dates."
Key point:
- Payroll coding needs current staff allocation information.
- Project allocations should be updated when roles and assignments change.

24. Q: What controls should exist over procurement coding before an invoice is posted?
A: Show answer
Procurement documents should establish the intended project, grant, account, and approval context before Finance receives the invoice for posting. The purchase request, purchase order, contract, or approval record can provide the primary coding evidence, while the invoice confirms what was actually billed. A three-way or equivalent review can determine whether the purchase, receipt, and invoice agree and whether the coding remains appropriate. Power Query can compare procurement and accounts-payable data, while validation logic can flag invoices with missing or inconsistent coding. This reduces the temptation to classify a transaction based solely on vendor description or invoice wording after the expenditure has already occurred.

Study instruction:
- "Trace the relationship between procurement approval, supporting documentation, and final accounting codes."
Key point:
- Coding should be supported by the approved purchasing context.
- Invoice processing should confirm rather than invent the project classification.

25. Q: How would you keep partner-reported expenditure aligned with the NGO's project and grant coding?
A: Show answer
Partner reports should be mapped into the same reporting dimensions used by the NGO, but the partner's original codes and descriptions should be preserved for traceability. I would first create a partner-specific crosswalk for account, project, grant, and activity classifications. Then I would validate that reported codes are permitted for the agreement and that totals reconcile to the supporting report or advance settlement. Power Query is useful where partners submit recurring files because transformation steps can standardize column names, dates, and classifications before aggregation. Exceptions such as unknown activity codes, new budget lines, or unexplained reallocations should be separately reviewed instead of silently mapped to a convenient category.

26. Q: How would you code donor receipts so funding information remains linked to the right grant or fund?
A: Show answer
Donor receipts should be captured with enough information to identify the relevant funding arrangement, grant, currency where applicable, receipt reference, and date. The coding should distinguish the cash event from the subsequent accounting treatment of the funding, because receipt, recognition, restriction, and reporting dimensions may not always be identical. A donor or grant master should contain the approved relationship between donor, award, fund, project, and reporting requirements. Power Query can match incoming bank receipts against the donor schedule and produce unmatched items for review. The control objective is to preserve the funding identity of the receipt without assuming that the bank narrative alone provides all necessary accounting information.

27. Q: Why should budget coding and actual transaction coding use the same structure?
A: Show answer
Budget-versus-actual analysis becomes unreliable when the budget uses one classification system and actual transactions use another. The budget should therefore be built around the same core project, grant, fund, and account dimensions used for actuals, or a documented mapping should exist where planning needs a different level of detail. This enables the reporting model to compare like with like. Power Query can load budget and actual data into one model and standardize their dimensions before analysis. If a budget line cannot map clearly to an actual account or project, that limitation should be visible rather than hidden inside a broad aggregation. Consistent structures improve both variance analysis and forecasting.

Study instruction:
- "Compare budget and actual structures and identify any dimension that cannot be matched reliably."
Key point:
- Budget and actual classifications should be compatible.
- Unmatched budget structures can weaken variance analysis.

28. Q: How would you prevent a transaction from being assigned to a grant that cannot fund its project?
A: Show answer
I would maintain an eligibility or relationship table showing which projects, activities, accounts, or other dimensions each grant is permitted to support. During processing, the transaction would be checked against that table rather than merely verifying that the grant code exists. Power Query can merge transactions with the relationship table and mark unauthorized combinations as exceptions. For user-entered transactions, data validation or dependent selection lists can reduce the probability of an invalid combination being selected in the first place. Finance should also review changes to grant-project relationships when agreements, budgets, amendments, or project structures change. The key is to encode known relationships into the control system rather than relying entirely on individual memory.

Study instruction:
- "Build a mental model of coding as both value validation and relationship validation."
Key point:
- Eligibility depends on the relationship among codes, not just their individual validity.
- Approved relationships should be maintained as controlled reference data.

29. Q: What is the purpose of a coding exception report?
A: Show answer
The exception report is designed to show the transactions that cannot safely pass through the normal coding workflow. Typical exceptions include missing project codes, inactive grants, invalid accounts, incompatible grant-project combinations, duplicate records, unclear source references, and allocation totals that do not reconcile. The report should show the transaction identifier, source, amount, affected dimension, reason for the exception, responsible person, status, and resolution date where appropriate. Power Query can generate the exception population after each refresh, while VBA can format it, assign workflow status, or export a review pack. A good exception report does not replace review; it concentrates review effort on records that need judgment.

Study instruction:
- "Practice designing exception reports around actionable fields rather than simply listing error messages."
Key point:
- Exception reporting focuses human attention where automated rules are insufficient.
- Each exception should have enough information for someone to resolve it.

30. Q: How would you decide whether a coding rule should be automated or reviewed manually?
A: Show answer
I would automate rules that are stable, objective, repeatable, and supported by reliable source data. Examples include matching a known project code to a master table, checking whether an account exists, or identifying duplicate source IDs. I would retain human review for ambiguous cases, judgment-based allocations, unusual transactions, new grants, unclear descriptions, and exceptions where the evidence is incomplete. Automation should reduce repetitive work without converting uncertain accounting judgments into invisible assumptions. A useful approach is to define confidence levels: automatic, review required, and blocked. VBA can enforce those statuses while Power Query performs the repeatable transformations. The system should make uncertainty visible rather than pretending every classification is deterministic.

Study instruction:
- "Identify which coding decisions are rule-based and which require professional judgment."
Key point:
- Automate predictable rules and escalate ambiguous cases.
- Good automation exposes uncertainty instead of concealing it.

31. Q: Why is it risky to use a default project or grant code for all uncoded transactions?
A: Show answer
A default code may make a workflow appear complete while silently distorting project and grant reporting. Once large numbers of transactions accumulate in the default category, it can become difficult to determine their true origin and expensive to correct them later. A controlled temporary code can sometimes be appropriate for genuinely pending information, but it should be clearly identified, limited, aged, and cleared through review. The system should also report how much value remains in temporary coding. Power Query can summarize pending items by age, source, and amount, while VBA can generate reminders or block final export when material unresolved exceptions remain. The purpose is to preserve uncertainty until the correct evidence is obtained, rather than replacing it with a convenient guess.

Study instruction:
- "Evaluate the difference between a controlled temporary classification and an uncontrolled default code."
Key point:
- Defaults can conceal missing information.
- Temporary classifications should be visible, aged, and cleared systematically.

32. Q: How would you monitor the quality of coding across the finance function?
A: Show answer
I would establish a small set of measurable data-quality indicators such as the percentage of transactions with valid codes, unresolved exceptions, invalid combinations, duplicate rates, manual overrides, and corrections made after month-end. The indicators should be segmented by source, team, transaction type, and period because a single overall percentage can hide a recurring weakness in one process. Trends are more useful than one isolated month. A dashboard can show whether coding quality is improving and where targeted intervention is needed. The purpose is not to create a performance score for individuals but to identify process weaknesses, training needs, and recurring control failures.

Study instruction:
- "Study how coding quality can be measured using process-level indicators rather than relying only on anecdotal review."
Key point:
- Data quality should be measured and trended.
- Segmentation helps identify where coding problems actually originate.

33. Q: What indicators would show that project and grant coding is deteriorating?
A: Show answer
Warning signs include a rising number of uncoded transactions, increasing use of temporary codes, more manual overrides, more post-close reclassifications, growing duplicate records, repeated invalid code combinations, and increasing differences between operational records and finance reports. Another warning sign is when staff begin maintaining private spreadsheets to "fix" coding outside the controlled system. The indicators should be reviewed with the relevant process owners because the root cause may be a changing source file, new projects, staff turnover, poor training, or a master-data problem. A useful control report should show both the number of exceptions and their financial value, since a small number of high-value errors may matter more than a large number of trivial ones.

Study instruction:
- "Learn to read coding-quality indicators as signals of underlying process problems rather than isolated spreadsheet errors."
Key point:
- Deterioration appears in exception trends and correction patterns.
- Frequency and financial impact should both be considered.

34. Q: How would you investigate repeated coding errors from one source system?
A: Show answer
I would first quantify the problem and identify the exact pattern: specific codes, users, project types, transaction classes, dates, or source fields. Then I would trace the issue upstream to determine whether the problem is caused by source-system configuration, mapping logic, user entry, outdated master data, or a change in business process. A sample of transactions should be compared with the underlying evidence rather than relying only on summarized exceptions. Once the cause is confirmed, the correction should be applied at the correct control point—for example, fixing the crosswalk rather than correcting thousands of output rows individually. Power Query is especially useful for testing revised mappings before deployment.

Study instruction:
- "Practice root-cause analysis that corrects the point of failure instead of repeatedly correcting symptoms."
Key point:
- Repeated errors require root-cause analysis.
- Fix the source, mapping, or control that creates the recurring error.

A: Show answer
I would create a controlled query for each source, standardize its columns and data types, preserve its source identifier, and then append or combine the normalized records into a common transaction table. When recurring files have the same structure, the Folder connector can combine them systematically instead of requiring manual copy-and-paste. After consolidation, I would merge the result with master coding tables to assign or validate the project, grant, fund, and account dimensions. Power Query's merge functionality can join tables using one or more matching columns, which is useful for coding crosswalks and master-data lookups. The resulting output should feed reporting and exception checks rather than becoming another manually edited working table.

Similar treatment can be applied to grant, fund, account, date, amount, and reference fields. The transformation should be documented and tested because a source-system change can otherwise break the query or produce incomplete output. When combining files, Microsoft recommends that files be compatible in structure for reliable automated processing, so source-folder controls and template requirements are also important. This approach keeps source differences in the transformation layer instead of spreading them throughout reporting formulas.

37. Q: Why should Power Query preserve the original source file name and source system?
A: Show answer
Source metadata is essential for troubleshooting and audit follow-up. When several files are combined, a reviewer needs to know whether a suspicious row came from payroll, procurement, a bank file, a partner report, or another source. It is also useful to know exactly which file contributed the record so a corrected source can be loaded without confusion. Power Query's folder-based processes expose file-system fields that can be retained as part of the combined dataset. I would therefore preserve fields such as source system, source file name, folder path where appropriate, source row identifier, and import or refresh date. This information creates a traceability layer between the consolidated table and the physical evidence used to create it.

38. Q: How would you use Excel tables to make a coding model more stable?
A: Show answer
I would store master codes, crosswalks, project lists, grant lists, allocation tables, and transaction inputs as structured Excel tables rather than loose cell ranges. Tables expand as new records are added and provide consistent field names for formulas and Power Query connections. This is particularly helpful for recurring monthly processes because new rows can be loaded without manually resetting range references. I would also avoid mixing notes, subtotals, blank spacer rows, or decorative formatting into the source tables. A clean table should represent one dataset with one header row and one record per row. This creates a reliable foundation for both automated transformation and human review.

39. Q: When would you use a Data Model rather than a single flat transaction table?
A: Show answer
A Data Model becomes useful when the finance process contains multiple related datasets such as transactions, project masters, grant masters, accounts, budgets, donors, staff, and calendar information. Keeping these as related tables avoids repeatedly copying descriptive fields into the transaction table and allows analysis across dimensions. For example, transactions can remain one fact table while projects and grants remain reference tables linked by keys. Microsoft notes that Excel Data Models can integrate multiple tables and support PivotTables and PivotCharts. This structure is useful when the organization needs consistent reporting across several dimensions without creating separate manually maintained reports for each combination.

40. Q: How would you handle a source file that suddenly changes its coding format?
A: Show answer
I would stop treating the change as a normal refresh and first identify exactly what changed. The query should be checked for missing columns, renamed fields, altered code formats, changed header positions, new file structures, or different data types. A controlled sample should be tested before the changed source is allowed into the official reporting process. Power Query's folder-combination approach works best when files have compatible structures, so incompatible files should be filtered or transformed before the main combination step. The finance team should document the change and update the source mapping rather than manually repairing the output each month. This prevents a one-off workaround from becoming a permanent hidden control weakness.

41. Q: What would you do if a project code is valid but the transaction date falls outside the project's active period?
A: Show answer
I would validate the code against both its identity and its effective dates. A project may remain in the master list for historical reporting while no longer being open for new transactions. The transaction should therefore be checked against the project's start and end dates or other approved validity rules. An out-of-period transaction is not automatically wrong because late postings and adjustments can occur, but it should be flagged for review rather than silently accepted. The reviewer should confirm whether the transaction relates to a prior-period service, correction, adjustment, or genuine activity outside the approved period. This is a good example of why status alone is insufficient; the transaction date and business context also matter.

Study instruction:

- Code validity can depend on transaction date.

42. Q: How would you manage coding for projects that close while transactions are still arriving?
A: Show answer
The project should remain historically identifiable even after operational activity ends, while new transactions should be evaluated against the project's closure policy. Some late invoices, accruals, reversals, or settlement items may legitimately relate to the closed project. I would therefore distinguish "historically valid" from "available for new operational spending." The coding master can record closure date and posting rules, and the exception report can flag transactions entered after closure. Finance should then classify those items based on evidence and the applicable close procedure. This is safer than simply deleting the project from the master list, because removal would break historical reporting and make older transactions harder to interpret.

Study instruction:

43. Q: How would you handle a transaction that needs to be reclassified from one project or grant to another after posting?

The documentation should identify the original coding, corrected coding, amount, reason, supporting evidence, approver, and effective date of the correction. The objective is not merely to make the current report look right but to preserve a traceable accounting history. Reclassification trends should also be reviewed because frequent corrections may indicate a weak upstream process. A workbook can maintain a reclassification log and use VBA to generate the corresponding journal preparation or approval checklist. Power Query can then include the corrected classification in reporting while retaining the underlying source and correction history.

44. Q: Why should manual coding overrides be logged?
A: Show answer
A manual override is a point where the normal automated or master-data rule did not determine the final classification. That makes the override important evidence for later review. The log should record the transaction, original automated result, final code, reason for override, person making or approving the decision, date, and supporting reference. Over time, the override log can reveal where the coding model is incomplete or where source data needs improvement. VBA can write override records automatically, while Power Query can summarize them by source, project, grant, account, and reason. Manual judgment remains possible, but it becomes visible, reviewable, and potentially improvable.

45. Q: How would you make coding changes traceable from source transaction to final report?

I would preserve a chain of identifiers through every stage: source identifier, normalized transaction identifier, coding result, exception status if any, adjustment or override reference, and final journal or reporting reference. Each transformation should change the data in a predictable way rather than replacing the original fields. A separate audit or processing log can record refresh dates, batch names, mapping versions, and material changes. Power Query can preserve source metadata and apply repeatable transformations, while VBA can write processing events into a controlled log. This creates a practical lineage from source evidence to published report and makes it easier to answer questions about why a transaction appears in a particular project or grant total.

46. Q: What documentation should support the organization's project and grant coding rules?
A: Show answer
The documentation should explain each coding dimension, what it means, when it should be used, who owns it, what supporting evidence is required, and what combinations are permitted. It should also explain how new codes are requested, how changes are approved, how inactive codes are handled, and how exceptions are resolved. Examples of correctly coded transactions are valuable because users often understand rules more quickly through practical cases. The coding guide should be version controlled so users know which rules apply at a particular date. Embedding a concise coding guide or control summary in the workbook can reduce dependence on informal instructions and support handover when staff change.

47. Q: Who should own the master project, grant, fund, and account coding lists?
A: Show answer
Ownership should be assigned clearly because a master list without an accountable owner quickly becomes outdated. The Finance function will often control accounting dimensions, while program, grants, or management teams may provide authoritative information about project or award status. The organization should define who requests a change, who validates it, who approves it, who implements it, and who monitors it. The owner does not necessarily have to perform every update, but responsibility for the quality of the master data should be unambiguous. A change log should preserve the approval and effective date of each material update. This creates governance around master data rather than treating it as an ordinary worksheet that anyone can edit.

Study instruction:
- "Define explicit ownership and approval responsibilities for every major coding dimension."
Key point:
- Master-data quality requires clear ownership.
- Request, approval, implementation, and review responsibilities should be separated where practical.
Source links:
- Create a Data Model in Excel — Microsoft Support
48. Q: How would you control requests for new project or grant codes?
A: Show answer
A new-code request should contain enough information to establish why the code is needed and how it fits into the existing structure. Depending on the coding model, the request could include project name, donor or grant relationship, start and end dates, responsible manager, funding source, reporting requirements, and the proposed account or activity relationships. Finance or the designated data owner should check for duplicates before creating a new code. Once approved, the code should be added to the master table with an effective date and status, and the relevant source-system crosswalks should be updated. This controlled process prevents users from inventing near-duplicate codes because they cannot find the approved one.

Study instruction:
- "Design a new-code request process that prevents duplicate and poorly defined master records."
Key point:
- New codes should be approved before they become usable transaction classifications.
- Duplicate checks should occur before creation.

- Merge Queries Overview — Microsoft Learn
- Apply Data Validation — Microsoft Support
49. Q: How would you handle a project name that is similar to an existing project but represents a different activity?
A: Show answer
I would not rely on names alone. I would compare the project owner, donor or grant relationship, start and end dates, geography, activity description, budget, and unique identifier before deciding whether the record is genuinely new or a duplicate. If it is a new project, it should receive a distinct code and the master data should make the difference clear through descriptions and metadata. If it is a duplicate of an existing project, the duplicate request should be rejected or merged under an approved data-management procedure rather than allowing both codes to circulate. Power Query can identify possible duplicates for review based on multiple fields, while human review confirms the business meaning.

Study instruction:
- "Practice using multiple identifying attributes to distinguish a genuine new project from a duplicate master-data record."
Key point:
- Similar names do not prove that two project records are the same.
- Duplicate prevention should combine automated detection with human confirmation.
Source links:
- Merge Queries Overview — Microsoft Learn
50. Q: What would you do when a source system contains a fund code that does not exist in the NGO's master list?
A: Show answer
I would first determine whether the source code represents a genuine new funding classification, a renamed legacy code, a data-entry error, or a mapping gap. The transaction should be kept visible and placed in an exception status while the issue is investigated. If it is a legitimate new code, the master list and crosswalk should be formally updated rather than creating a one-off formula that handles the value secretly. If it is an old or incorrect code, the source mapping should be corrected with evidence. Power Query can surface all unmatched values after each refresh, which makes this type of master-data control repeatable. The key is to distinguish a missing master record from an invalid transaction classification.

Study instruction:
- "Learn to classify unmatched codes as either master-data gaps, legitimate new values, or transaction errors."
Key point:
- An unmatched code requires investigation before classification.
- Permanent mapping changes should be documented in the master-data process.
Source links:
- Merge Queries Overview — Microsoft Learn
51. Q: How would you reconcile project and grant coding totals back to the general ledger?
A: Show answer
I would create a reconciliation that compares the detailed coded transaction population with the corresponding general-ledger totals for the same period and scope. The reconciliation should be performed before relying on project or grant reports for management or donor reporting. Differences may come from excluded accounts, timing, journals not included in the source extract, duplicated data, incorrect filters, or mapping errors. The workbook should show the difference explicitly and require resolution before the report is treated as complete. Power Query can consolidate transaction detail and reconciliation data, while VBA can generate a standardized reconciliation summary. The critical control is that project and grant reporting must remain explainable back to the accounting records.

Study instruction:
- "Practice reconciling detailed dimension-level reports to the general ledger and investigating every material difference."
Key point:
- Dimension reports should reconcile to the underlying accounting records.
- Reconciliation differences need explanation, not silent adjustment.

- Create a Data Model in Excel — Microsoft Support
- Merge Queries Overview — Microsoft Learn
52. Q: Why should project, grant, fund, and account coding be reconciled before donor reporting?
A: Show answer
Donor reports depend on the organization's ability to show that reported expenditure belongs to the correct award and activity. If the underlying transaction coding has not been reconciled, a report may contain totals that cannot be traced cleanly to the accounting records or supporting documentation. The review should therefore confirm completeness, valid code combinations, corrections, excluded items, and agreement with the ledger before figures are finalized. A common reporting model makes it possible to use the same transaction population for project, grant, fund, and account views rather than rebuilding donor reports from separate spreadsheets. This reduces the risk of inconsistent totals appearing in different reports for the same period.

Study instruction:
- "Review donor reporting as an output of controlled accounting data rather than as an independent spreadsheet exercise."
Key point:
- Donor reports should be derived from reconciled and controlled transaction data.
- One validated transaction population reduces conflicting report totals.

- Create a Data Model in Excel — Microsoft Support
53. Q: How would you prevent coding errors from being introduced when data is copied between workbooks?
A: Show answer
The strongest approach is to minimize manual copy-and-paste and instead connect the workbooks through controlled tables or repeatable imports. When manual transfer is unavoidable, the source and destination structures should be fixed, with validation rules and a clear handoff procedure. Power Query can import source tables directly and standardize them without retyping values. If VBA is used for transfer or export, it should validate required dimensions before generating the output and should preserve the source reference. A review step should compare record counts and totals before and after the transfer. The aim is to reduce the number of uncontrolled points where someone can change a project or grant code during copying.

Study instruction:
- "Identify every manual handoff in the current process and determine whether it can be replaced by a controlled import."
Key point:
- Manual re-entry creates avoidable coding risk.
- Automated transfer should still include validation and reconciliation.
Source links:
- Power Query Excel Connector — Microsoft Learn
- Excel VBA Reference — Microsoft Learn
54. Q: What role should VBA play in maintaining coding accuracy?
A: Show answer
VBA is most useful for workflow enforcement and repetitive control activities rather than for replacing accounting judgment. It can validate required fields, run standardized checks, generate exception reports, create review packs, log overrides, assign batch references, and prevent exports when mandatory controls have not been completed. It can also provide buttons that guide users through a defined processing sequence. However, VBA should not contain critical coding rules only in hidden code if the rules can reasonably be maintained in visible reference tables. Keeping mappings and master data in structured tables makes them easier to review and update, while VBA orchestrates the process around them. This separation improves maintainability and reduces the risk that a coding rule is hidden inside a difficult-to-review macro.

Study instruction:
- "Learn to use VBA as workflow control around transparent master data rather than as a hidden repository of accounting policy."
Key point:
- VBA is valuable for validation, workflow, logging, and reporting.
- Core coding rules should remain understandable and maintainable.

55. Q: How would you design a monthly coding review process across several transaction sources?
A: Show answer
I would begin with a defined source inventory so the team knows which datasets must arrive each month. After import, the process should standardize fields, validate master codes, test combinations, check duplicates, identify missing coding, reconcile totals, review overrides, and resolve exceptions. The final stage should confirm that the coded transaction population agrees with the ledger and that unresolved exceptions are documented. A status tracker can show which source files are received, refreshed, validated, reviewed, and cleared. Power Query can perform repeatable consolidation and checks, while VBA can generate the monthly review pack and status summary. A clear sequence matters because coding quality is easier to maintain when every month follows the same control cycle.

Study instruction:
- "Memorize the recurring control sequence from source receipt through coding, exception resolution, reconciliation, and final clearance."
Key point:
- A consistent monthly workflow reduces missed coding checks.
- Source completeness and final reconciliation are both part of coding control.

56. Q: What evidence should be retained when a project or grant code is changed manually?
A: Show answer
The record should preserve the original coding, corrected coding, reason for the change, supporting document or reference, date of change, and person who made or approved the correction. Depending on the organization's workflow, the evidence may include an approved reclassification, manager confirmation, amended budget, grant correspondence, contract, or source-document correction. The correction should remain linked to the original transaction rather than overwriting the history with no explanation. A reclassification log can provide a central record of such changes and can be analyzed for recurring patterns. This turns coding correction into a controlled accounting process rather than an invisible spreadsheet edit.

Study instruction:
- "Review the minimum evidence needed to make a manual coding correction traceable and reviewable."
Key point:
- Manual changes need a reason, evidence, and accountable approval.
- Original and corrected classifications should both remain traceable.

57. Q: How would you train non-finance staff to enter project and grant codes correctly?
A: Show answer
Training should focus on decisions users actually make rather than teaching the entire chart of accounts. Staff should understand what project and grant codes mean, which codes they are authorized to use, where to find the approved list, what evidence is required, and what to do when the correct code is unclear. Short scenario-based examples are often more useful than long lists of definitions. The input template should also guide users through validated selections where possible. Finance should monitor the error patterns after training so the guidance can be improved. If users repeatedly make the same coding mistake, the solution may be to redesign the form or master data rather than simply repeat the training.

Study instruction:
- "Build training around real transaction examples and the decisions users must make before submitting data."
Key point:
- Training should teach practical classification decisions and escalation routes.
- Repeated mistakes may require better system design, not only more instruction.
Source links:
- Apply Data Validation — Microsoft Support
58. Q: How would you explain coding requirements to program managers who are not accounting specialists?
A: Show answer
I would explain the business purpose of each dimension rather than beginning with accounting terminology. For example, a project code answers "which activity is this for?", a grant code answers "which award or funding arrangement does this relate to?", and an account code answers "what type of transaction is this?" Program managers should also understand that accurate coding affects budget monitoring, donor reporting, and management decisions. Finance can provide a short coding guide and show common examples and exceptions relevant to the program. The objective is not to turn program staff into accountants but to make the information they supply sufficiently precise for Finance to record and report it correctly.

Study instruction:
- "Practice explaining coding in operational language that connects each field to a real management purpose."
Key point:
- Good coding guidance explains why information is needed, not just what value to enter.
- Non-finance users need practical boundaries and escalation guidance.
Source links:
- Apply Data Validation — Microsoft Support
59. Q: How would you handle a disagreement between Finance and a program manager about the correct project code?
A: Show answer
The discussion should return to evidence and the approved coding rule rather than personal preference. Finance should identify the accounting or reporting requirement, while the program manager can explain the operational purpose of the expenditure. Relevant evidence may include the approved budget, purchase request, grant agreement, activity plan, contract, or project documentation. If the rule is genuinely ambiguous, the matter should be escalated to the designated coding owner or policy authority and the final interpretation should be documented for future cases. The resolution should then be incorporated into the coding guide or master-data logic if the issue is likely to recur. A disagreement is often an opportunity to improve the clarity of the coding framework.

Study instruction:
- "Use evidence, approved rules, and escalation rather than individual preference to resolve coding disputes."
Key point:
- Coding disagreements should be resolved against documented requirements.
- Recurring ambiguity should trigger an improvement to the coding framework.
Source links:
- Merge Queries Overview — Microsoft Learn
60. Q: How would you know that the coding system is working effectively over time?
A: Show answer
An effective coding system should produce reliable reporting with relatively few unresolved exceptions, limited manual overrides, stable reconciliation results, and clear traceability from source transactions to project, grant, fund, and account reports. It should also adapt to new projects, grants, and source systems without requiring large numbers of manual workarounds. I would review trend measures such as exception volume, financial value of unresolved items, duplicate rates, reclassification frequency, mapping changes, and time required to complete the monthly coding review. The most useful outcome is not simply a low error count; it is a process in which errors are detected early, corrected with evidence, and used to improve the underlying controls. A well-designed Power Query model, structured reference tables, and VBA-supported review workflow can make these measurements repeatable.

Study instruction:
- "Evaluate coding effectiveness through trends in accuracy, exceptions, reconciliation, traceability, and maintenance effort."
Key point:
- Coding effectiveness should be measured through outcomes and process stability.
- A strong system detects, explains, and learns from errors rather than merely hiding them.
